\end{itemize}

\chapter{Fireteam Index Prediction Summary}

This chapter summarizes the accuracy of the Fireteam Index prediction algorithms across all games in this collection.

\section{Overall Results}

\begin{tabular}{lr}
\toprule
\textbf{Metric} & \textbf{Value} \\
\midrule
Total games & 16 \\
Decisive games & 12 \\
Draws (both sides analyzed) & 4 \\
Games with prediction & 16 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\subsection{Standard FTI (Weights: 1.0, 1.0, 1.0, 0.1)}

\begin{tabular}{lcc}

\subsection{Truncated FTI (Weights: 1.2, 0.0, 0.0, 0.6)}

The truncated FTI uses only Space and Threats components, testing whether a simpler metric focusing on territorial control and tactical pressure is more predictive.

\begin{tabular}{lcc}

\subsection{Comparison}

Both FTI variants show similar predictive accuracy (standard: 68.8\%, truncated: 68.8\%), suggesting that the additional Mobility and King Safety components provide marginal benefit.


\section{Interpretation}

These predictions tracked the eventual winner in each decisive game. 
A ``WIN'' prediction means the Fireteam Index exceeded the threshold for the required number of consecutive plies, indicating sustained positional dominance.

The per-ply algorithm uses raw positional deltas, while the windowed algorithm smooths values over a 10-ply (5 full move) rolling average to reduce noise.

\appendix
\chapter{Methodology: How Metrics Are Computed}

This appendix explains how the positional metrics are computed.


This section explains how Stockfish's classical evaluation computes each positional factor. Note that modern Stockfish primarily uses NNUE (neural network) evaluation, but the classical evaluation terms provide interpretable metrics for educational purposes.

\section{Space}

Space evaluation counts squares in the center (files c-f, ranks 2-4 for White, 5-7 for Black) that are:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Attacked by at least one friendly pawn, AND
\item Not occupied by any enemy piece
\end{enumerate}

The computation is weighted by the number of pieces behind the pawn chain---this rewards positions where you control territory AND have pieces to exploit that space. The space term is scaled by total piece count; it matters more in closed positions with many pieces.

\section{Mobility}

Mobility counts legal moves for each piece type, with different weights:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Knights}: Safe squares attacked (excluding squares defended by enemy pawns)
\item \textbf{Bishops}: Safe squares on diagonals (bonus for long, unobstructed diagonals)
\item \textbf{Rooks}: Squares on files/ranks (bonus for open files, 7th rank penetration)
\item \textbf{Queens}: Combination of bishop and rook mobility patterns
\end{itemize}

``Safe squares" exclude those defended by enemy pawns or where exchanges would lose material. Mobility in the opponent's territory is valued higher than mobility in your own.

\section{King Safety}

King safety combines several factors:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Pawn shield}: Pawns on 2nd/3rd rank directly in front of the castled king
\item \textbf{King tropism}: Distance of enemy pieces to your king (closer = more dangerous)
\item \textbf{Attack units}: Count of enemy pieces attacking squares adjacent to your king
\item \textbf{Safe checks}: Number of safe checking squares available to the opponent
\end{itemize}

\section{Threats}

Threat evaluation considers:
\begin{itemize}
\item Hanging pieces (attacked but not adequately defended)
\item Safe pawn advances that create threats
\item Minor pieces attacking major pieces
\item Weak squares in the pawn structure
\end{itemize}

\section{Middlegame vs Endgame (MG/EG)}

Each term has separate middlegame (MG) and endgame (EG) values. Stockfish blends these based on remaining material (the ``phase"). With all pieces on the board, MG dominates; as pieces trade, EG values become more important. For example, space matters greatly in the middlegame but is nearly irrelevant in endgames.

\section{Game Character Classification}

Games are classified based on evaluation volatility and directionality. Let $m_2$ be the maximum evaluation for White during the game (in pawns) and $m_1$ be the minimum. The \emph{spread} $d = m_2 - m_1$ measures the total evaluation range.

\paragraph{Spread Classifications.} Based on the magnitude of $d$:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Balanced} ($d < 1$): Minimal advantage shifts; controlled, technical play with neither side gaining a significant edge.
\item \textbf{Tense} ($1 \le d < 3$): Normal competitive tension; typical of well-played games where small advantages are contested.
\item \textbf{Tactical} ($3 \le d < 6$): Significant evaluation swings; tactical complications, missed opportunities, or speculative play.
\item \textbf{Chaotic} ($d \ge 6$): Wild swings in evaluation; likely includes major blunders, speculative sacrifices, or complex tactical melees.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Directionality Classifications.} Based on whether the advantage changed hands:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{One-sided}: Either $m_1 > -\frac{1}{2}$ (White always comfortable) or $m_2 < \frac{1}{2}$ (Black always comfortable). The leading side maintained control throughout.
\item \textbf{Seesaw}: Both $m_1 < -1$ and $m_2 > 1$. The advantage genuinely changed hands, with each side holding a significant advantage at some point.
\item \textbf{Normal}: Neither one-sided nor seesaw; moderate swings without complete reversals.
\end{itemize}

These classifications combine to describe the overall game character. For example, a ``tactical seesaw battle" indicates significant evaluation swings ($3 \le d < 6$) with the advantage changing hands multiple times.

\section{Fireteam Index (FTI) and Win Prediction}

The Fireteam Index is a composite metric inspired by the ancient mathematical board game \textit{Rithmomachia}, where a ``progression victory'' is achieved when a coordinated group of pieces (a ``fireteam'') establishes control deep in enemy territory. Similarly, in chess, sustained positional dominance across multiple factors often precedes victory.

\paragraph{Standard FTI Formula.} The standard Fireteam Index combines all four positional metrics into a single value measuring overall positional advantage from a given player's perspective:

\begin{equation}
\text{FT}_{\text{std}} = 1.0 \cdot \Delta\text{Space} + 1.0 \cdot \Delta\text{Mobility} + 1.0 \cdot \Delta\text{King Safety} + 0.1 \cdot \Delta\text{Threats}
\end{equation}

where each $\Delta$ represents (Player's value $-$ Opponent's value). Positive FT indicates the player has overall positional superiority; negative indicates the opponent is better.

The Threats component is weighted at 0.1 because its raw values are typically an order of magnitude larger than the other metrics, and empirical testing showed this weighting provides better predictive accuracy.

\paragraph{Truncated FTI Formula.} The truncated Fireteam Index uses only Space and Threats, testing whether a simpler metric focusing on territorial control and tactical pressure is more predictive for decisive games:

\begin{equation}
\text{FT}_{\text{trunc}} = 1.2 \cdot \Delta\text{Space} + 0.6 \cdot \Delta\text{Threats}
\end{equation}

This variant hypothesizes that Mobility and King Safety may introduce noise that obscures the core signal of who controls territory and applies pressure. The increased weight on Space (1.2 vs 1.0) and Threats (0.6 vs 0.1) compensates for the removed components.

\paragraph{Prediction Algorithms.} Two algorithms are used to predict game outcomes based on FT (applied independently to both the standard and truncated variants):

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Per-Ply (Raw)}: Examines the raw FT value at each ply after the opening phase (ply 16, approximately 8 moves). If FT exceeds zero for 10 consecutive plies (5 full moves), a WIN is predicted for that player.

\item \textbf{Windowed (Smoothed)}: Computes a 10-ply rolling average of FT before applying the same streak detection. This smooths out tactical noise and captures sustained trends rather than momentary fluctuations.
\end{enumerate}

Both algorithms predict DRAW if no player achieves the required streak of positional dominance.

\paragraph{Interpretation.} The FT prediction measures \emph{positional} dominance, which is a necessary but not sufficient condition for winning. A player may dominate positionally yet fail to convert due to:
\begin{itemize}
\item Tactical errors (blunders, missed combinations)
\item Time pressure in rapid/blitz games
\item Tenacious defense by the opponent
\item Drawing tendencies in certain structures (opposite-colored bishops, fortresses)
\end{itemize}

Conversely, a player may win without ever achieving sustained FT dominance through a single tactical strike or opponent error. The FT metric is most predictive in games where positional understanding---rather than pure calculation---determines the outcome.

\paragraph{For Draws.} When analyzing drawn games, the FT is computed from both players' perspectives. This reveals whether:
\begin{itemize}
\item Neither side achieved dominance (a ``true draw'' with balanced play)
\item One side dominated but failed to convert (a ``missed win'')
\item Both sides dominated at different points (a ``mutual chances draw'')
\end{itemize}
